Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.

I’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.

I’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.

But then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.

It’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.

I’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.

Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do these eating episodes happen?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Since I’ve been home with my back injury, it’s been happening several nights most weeks; I can’t think of a single week in these three months where I didn’t have at least one of those episodes.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.

These past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.

I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.

Sometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.

There have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.

Sometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.

So… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”

Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.\n\nI’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. couple tamales, chips, and whatever leftovers are sitting there like I’m feeding a whole crew instead of just myself.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start, it feels like the same discipline I had on the road just shuts off, and even while I’m telling myself, 'Magin, put the fork down,' my hand keeps going back for more.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I eat so fast it’s like I’m on a tight delivery schedule, barely tasting it and sometimes catching myself breathing hard because I’m shoveling food in without slowing down.\n- key: S052\n  name: Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full\n  description: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n  manifestation: I’ll keep eating until my stomach is stretched and hurting, and with this bad back I end up stuck in the recliner feeling miserable because even getting up feels like too much.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that after I do it, I sit there looking at the empty containers and I feel disgusted with myself, like I’m not the steady, in-control man I’ve always tried to be.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Since I’ve been home with my back injury, it’s been happening several nights most weeks; I can’t think of a single week in these three months where I didn’t have at least one of those episodes.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’ve started avoiding check-in calls from the drivers I was supposed to mentor because I feel ashamed History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.\n\nI’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. I’m still not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m not trying to. I just haven’t been paying close enough attention to my desire for sex in a way where I can say, yes, it’s definitely gone down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.\n\nI’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed that happening since the eating episodes started.\n\nThat’s one of those things where I feel a little awkward answering because I haven’t been, you know, keeping track of it in a straightforward way. It hasn’t stood out to me like, “Oh, this started when the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no clear difficulty that I’ve noticed, or at least nothing I can confidently say is tied to this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire for sex?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure, to be honest. I don’t think I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I haven’t noticed a definite problem with sexual performance or function that started when the eating episodes started, at least not in a way that’s obvious to me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.\n\nI’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I’m not completely sure. I mean, I can see how emotions might have something to do with it sometimes, but I haven’t really sat down and tracked it like, “Okay, I felt this way, and then I ate.” So I don’t want to say yes like I’m certain.

But I don’t know if I’d call that the usual trigger, because other times it feels like it just happens because food is there, or because I’m alone, or because the day got away from me. It’s hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just habit or convenience or not paying attention.

So… maybe sometimes, but I can’t honestly say they’re usually triggered by feeling emotional. I haven’t noticed a clear enough pattern.

I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining an erection during sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed that happening since the eating episodes started.\n\nThat’s one of those things where I feel a little awkward answering because I haven’t been, you know, keeping track of it in a straightforward way. If there’s been a time here or there where things weren’t working the way I’d want, I don’t know that I connected it to the eating or thought of it as a pattern. It hasn’t stood out to me like, “Oh, this started when the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no clear difficulty that I’ve noticed, or at least nothing I can confidently say is tied to this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire for sex?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m not trying to. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with sexual performance or function since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can give you a clean yes or no on that. Nothing has stood out enough for me to say, “This is a problem now,” or “This clearly changed after the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear issue, but I’m not completely sure because it’s not something I’ve been paying a lot of direct attention to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with ejaculation since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I’ve noticed anything specific with ejaculation. Like, I can’t say there’s been a clear difficulty that started after the eating episodes began.\n\nIt’s kind of the same as the other sexual questions, where I feel a little embarrassed because I haven’t been monitoring it in a detailed way, and it’s not something I’ve been sitting down and thinking, “Okay, is this different now?” If there were an occasional time where things didn’t happen exactly normally, I don’t know that I’d connect it to the eating, or even remember it as a pattern.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a clear problem with ejaculation since then. At least nothing that stands out to me enough to say yes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, I can see how emotions might have something to do with it sometimes, but I haven’t really sat down and tracked it like, “Okay, I felt this way, and then I ate.” So I don’t want to say yes like I’m certain.\n\nThere are times when I’m having a rough day or I’m irritated or just kind of tense, and eating can sort of happen without me thinking it through very much. But I don’t know if I’d call that the usual trigger, because other times it feels like it just happens because food is there, or because I’m alone, or because the day got away from me. It’s hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just habit or convenience or not paying attention.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t honestly say they’re usually triggered by feeling emotional. I haven’t noticed a clear enough pattern.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your eating episodes tend to happen when you're not interested in sex?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve noticed that kind of connection, honestly. Like, I don’t think I’ve ever thought, “I’m not interested in sex right now, and that’s when I end up eating.” That’s not a pattern that jumps out at me.

I guess there are times when I’m just not feeling very connected to anything, or I’m tired or distracted, and then food becomes kind of the thing I focus on, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being interested in sex specifically. I haven’t really been comparing those two things in my head.

So… no, I can’t really say the eating episodes tend to happen when I’m not interested in sex. I just haven’t noticed that clearly.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. I’ll be sitting there in my truck or at home, looking at the empty containers or wrappers, and it just hits me all at once. I feel disgusted with myself. Not just regret, but like… ashamed, like I don’t even recognize myself in that moment.\n\nI’ve always tried to be a steady kind of man, someone in control, someone who keeps things together. And after it happens, I’m just sitting there staring at the evidence of it, thinking, “What are you doing?” It makes me feel like I’m not the person I’ve always tried to be. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s probably the scariest part to admit, honestly.\n\nI’ve spent a lot of my life being disciplined, you know? Driving, keeping schedules, doing what needs to be done, staying steady. But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty, ashamed, disgusted—kind of all of it mixed together.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve stopped and I’m just sitting there, and I can see what I did. Empty containers, wrappers, plates, whatever it is. That’s when it really lands on me. I’ll think, “How did you let this happen again?” And it’s not just feeling bad about eating too much. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining an erection during sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed that happening since the eating episodes started.\n\nThat’s one of those things where I feel a little awkward answering because I haven’t been, you know, keeping track of it in a straightforward way. If there’s been a time here or there where things weren’t working the way I’d want, I don’t know that I connected it to the eating or thought of it as a pattern. It hasn’t stood out to me like, “Oh, this started when the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no clear difficulty that I’ve noticed, or at least nothing I can confidently say is tied to this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire for sex?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m not trying to. I just haven’t been paying close enough attention to my desire for sex in a way where I can say, yes, it’s definitely gone down.\n\nSome days I’m more withdrawn or in my own head, and on those days sex isn’t exactly something I’m focused on. But I don’t know if that’s a true decrease in desire or just me being distracted or not feeling great about myself. It hasn’t jumped out at me as a clear change, like, “Oh, I don’t want sex anymore,” or anything that definite.\n\nSo I’d say I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with sexual performance or function since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I haven’t noticed a definite problem with sexual performance or function that started when the eating episodes started, at least not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nI mean, there are times where I’m not exactly feeling great about myself or I’m distracted, and I’m sure that can affect how present I am, but as far as an actual performance issue or something physically not working right, I haven’t been tracking that closely. Nothing has stood out enough for me to say, “This is a problem now,” or “This clearly changed after the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear issue, but I’m not completely sure because it’s not something I’ve been paying a lot of direct attention to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with ejaculation since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I’ve noticed anything specific with ejaculation. Like, I can’t say there’s been a clear difficulty that started after the eating episodes began.\n\nIt’s kind of the same as the other sexual questions, where I feel a little embarrassed because I haven’t been monitoring it in a detailed way, and it’s not something I’ve been sitting down and thinking, “Okay, is this different now?” If there were an occasional time where things didn’t happen exactly normally, I don’t know that I’d connect it to the eating, or even remember it as a pattern.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve had a clear problem with ejaculation since then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know that I eat more because of that, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my eating in relation to sex drive or interest, so I’d be guessing if I said there’s a connection.

There are times where I’m not thinking about sex at all and I might overeat, but that doesn’t necessarily mean the lack of interest is causing it. A lot of the time, the eating just feels like its own thing—like I’m in the kitchen, or I’ve got food around, or I’m alone, and it kind of builds from there. I’m not usually making that connection in my mind like, “Oh, I’m not interested in sex, so now I’m eating more.”

So I’d say I haven’t noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happening, I’m not aware of it in a clear way.

But when one of those eating episodes starts, it’s like that whole part of me just shuts off. I’ll be sitting there actually telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. Stop now.” And I hear it in my own head clear as day.\n\nBut then my hand just goes back for more anyway. Like there’s a gap between what I’m telling myself and what I’m doing. I’m not blacked out or anything—I know what’s happening—but it feels like I can’t get the brakes to catch once I’m rolling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. If there’s been a time here or there where things weren’t working the way I’d want, I don’t know that I connected it to the eating or thought of it as a pattern. I wish I could say it was just once in a while, but it’s not.\n\nThese past three months, I honestly can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen at least once. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time. That’s what’s confusing about it.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m hungry at first, but when I really pay attention, it’s not like my stomach is growling or I’ve gone all day without food. It’s more like… I get this pull toward eating, and I don’t always know where it’s coming from. I’ll be sitting there and food will just start sounding like the only thing I can think about, but I’m not sure it’s actual hunger in the normal sense.\n\nThere have been times I’ve eaten a regular meal and then later still ended up doing it anyway, so that makes me think it can’t just be being hungry. But in the moment, I don’t stop and sort it out very well. I just kind of go with it, and only afterward I’m thinking, “Was I even hungry?” A lot of times the honest answer is probably no, or at least I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these eating episodes are triggered by feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed, “Oh, I’m stressed, so now I’m eating.” It doesn’t always feel that clean or obvious to me.\n\nSometimes I’m upset or wound up and then later it happens, but I don’t know if that’s the cause or if it’s just part of the same bad evening, if that makes sense. Other times I don’t feel especially emotional beforehand—I’m just sitting there, and then the idea of food gets in my head and it sort of takes over.\n\nSo… maybe stress or feeling down has something to do with it sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve tracked it well enough to know. I haven’t noticed one clear trigger where I can say, “Yes, that’s what sets it off.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any issues with your sex life around the time these eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I haven’t noticed a clear connection, I guess. It’s not something I’ve been tracking the way I’ve been noticing the eating at night.\n\nI mean, I know things haven’t exactly felt normal overall since I’ve been dealing with all of this and being home more, but as far as a specific sex-life issue starting right when the eating got worse, I can’t honestly point to something and say, “Yes, that changed then.” I’d have to think about it more. It’s not something that’s been front and center in my mind compared with the food stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a decrease in your interest in sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying close enough attention to say there’s been a definite decrease because of the eating episodes.\n\nI mean, it’s hard because when I’m feeling bad about myself or just kind of preoccupied with food and all that, I’m not exactly in the best headspace, but I can’t separate it out and say, “Yes, my interest in sex went down when this started.” It hasn’t stood out to me that clearly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed a clean before-and-after kind of change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex lately?\n\nPatient: I guess I still don’t really know how to pin that down. Lately, my mind’s been kind of crowded with everything around the eating and feeling off in general, so sex hasn’t been something I’ve been thinking about in a very clear way.\n\nBut I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I’m definitely less interested,” because I haven’t been measuring it or noticing it like that. It’s more like… it’s not the main thing on my mind, but I don’t know if that means my interest is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and not feeling great about myself. So I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining an erection during sex since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t say I’ve clearly noticed that happening since the eating episodes started.\n\nThat’s one of those things where I feel a little awkward answering because I haven’t been, you know, keeping track of it in a straightforward way. If there’s been a time here or there where things weren’t working the way I’d want, I don’t know that I connected it to the eating or thought of it as a pattern. It hasn’t stood out to me like, “Oh, this started when the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no clear difficulty that I’ve noticed, or at least nothing I can confidently say is tied to this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your desire for sex?\n\nPatient: I’m still not really sure, to be honest. I know that probably sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m not trying to. I just haven’t been paying close enough attention to my desire for sex in a way where I can say, yes, it’s definitely gone down.\n\nSome days I’m more withdrawn or in my own head, and on those days sex isn’t exactly something I’m focused on. But I don’t know if that’s a true decrease in desire or just me being distracted or not feeling great about myself. It hasn’t jumped out at me as a clear change, like, “Oh, I don’t want sex anymore,” or anything that definite.\n\nSo I’d say I haven’t really noticed a clear decrease. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with sexual performance or function since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I haven’t noticed a definite problem with sexual performance or function that started when the eating episodes started, at least not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nI mean, there are times where I’m not exactly feeling great about myself or I’m distracted, and I’m sure that can affect how present I am, but as far as an actual performance issue or something physically not working right, I haven’t been tracking that closely. Nothing has stood out enough for me to say, “This is a problem now,” or “This clearly changed after the eating got worse.”\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear issue, but I’m not completely sure because it’s not something I’ve been paying a lot of direct attention to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulty with ejaculation since these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I’ve noticed anything specific with ejaculation. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is what happens after I eat like that. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. It feels more personal than that, like I’ve failed at being the kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as steady. Controlled. Someone who handles things, who keeps moving, who doesn’t fall apart over something like food. So afterward, I feel like I don’t even match up with who I’m supposed to be. I just sit there feeling disgusted with myself, and honestly it’s hard to even look at the mess sometimes because it feels like evidence against me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks. Some weeks it’s more than that—like a few nights where I end up doing it again, even after telling myself earlier in the day that I wasn’t going to. It’s become this thing I almost dread in the evenings, because I know if I’m home and not moving around much, there’s a good chance it’ll happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d call it real hunger, not most of the time.
